<margin>page 14 and following</margin>my own part it was ever my desire to bury these
things in silence, or to paint them out in sha-
dows <note>In the context of 17th-century alchemy, to "paint in shadows" meant to describe secrets using metaphors, allegories, or "decked" language to hide them from the uninitiated.</note>, but I have spoken thus clearly, and o-
penly out of the *affection* I bear to some, who
have deserved much more at my hands. True it
is, I intended sometimes to expose a greater
work to the world, which I promised in my
*Anthroposophia* <note>Thomas Vaughan’s first published work, *Anthroposophia Theomagica* (1650), which explored the magical and spiritual nature of humanity.</note>, but I have been since acquain-
ted with that world, and I found it base, and
<margin>this is mean, and <unclear>sophistical</unclear></margin>unworthy: wherefore I shall keep in my first
happy *solitudes*, for *noise* is *nothing* to me,
I seek not any man's *applause*. If it be the will
of my *God* to call me forth, and that it may
make for the *honor* of his *name*, in that
respect I may write again, for I fear not the
*judgment* of *man*, but in the interim here
shall be an end.

->THE END.<-
<note>original: "FINIS."</note>

<summary>In this concluding passage, Thomas Vaughan warns against disrupting the harmony of nature and explains his decision to remain in solitary study rather than publishing further works for an unworthy public.</summary>
<keywords>Nature, Devil's design, Anthroposophia Theomagica, solitude, Thomas Vaughan, alchemical ethics, Zoroastrian oracles</keywords>

--- Page 191 ---
<meta>This page contains a concluding poem, likely following the main text of Thomas Vaughan's "Caelum Terrae." The author transitions from the heavy philosophical and alchemical discourse of the previous pages to a lighter, more personal address to his own work as it goes out into the world.</meta>

And now my Book, let it not stop thy Flight,
That thy just Author, is not Lord, or Knight.
I can define myself: and have the Art
Still to present one face, and still one Heart.
But for nine years some Great ones cannot see
What they have been, nor know they what to be.
What though I have no Rattles <note>In this context, "rattles" refers to hollow, noisy titles of nobility or empty honors that carry no real substance.</note> to my name,
Do’st hold a simple Honesty no Fame?
Or art thou such a stranger to the Times,
Thou can’st not know my Fortunes from my Crimes. <note>Vaughan suggests that in the chaotic political climate of the 17th century, a person’s simple misfortune or poverty was often unfairly judged as a sign of criminal character or divine disfavor.</note>
Go forth, and fear not: some will gladly be
Thy learned friends, whom I did never see.
Nor should’st thou fear thy welcome: thy small Price
Cannot undo ’em, though they pay Excise. <note>A tax on domestic goods. Vaughan is noting that his book is inexpensive enough for people to buy even during times of high taxation.</note>
Thy Bulk’s not great: it will not much distress
Their empty Pockets, but their Studies less.
Th’art no Galeon <note>original: "Galeon"; a large sailing ship. Here used metaphorically for massive, heavy scholarly volumes (folios) that are difficult to move or read outside of a library.</note>, as Books of Burthen be,
Which cannot ride but in a Library.
Th’art a fine Thing and little: it may Chance
Ladies will buy thee for a new Romance. <note>In the 1600s, a "romance" was a popular work of fiction involving adventure and courtly love; Vaughan suggests his book is small and attractive enough to be read for pleasure.</note>
Oh how I’le envy Thee! when thou art spread
In the bright Sun-shine of their Eyes, and read
With Breath of Amber, Lips of Rose, that lend
Perfumes unto thy Leaves, shall never spend:
When from their white hands they shall let thee fall
Into their Bosoms, which I may not call
Ought of Misfortune, Thou do’st drop to rest
In a more pleasing place, and art more blest.

<meta>catchword: There</meta>

<vocab>Author, Rattles, Honesty, Crimes, Excise, Galleon, Library, Romance, Amber, Bosoms, Misfortune</vocab>

<summary>This page contains a concluding poem where the author addresses his book as it is sent to the printer, defending his own lack of noble status and humble character. He playfully imagines the book being cherished by readers, particularly women, as a small and portable volume compared to heavy academic texts.</summary>
<keywords>authorship, social status, book trade, 17th-century taxes, metaphors, poetic address, Thomas Vaughan</keywords>

--- Page 192 ---
<meta>This page concludes the author's address to his book, transitioning from the previous page's discussion of the book's physical size and value to a defense of its plain style and its purpose as a vessel for Truth. It serves as a final epilogue to the work.</meta>

There in some silken, soft fold you shall lie
Hidden like their love, or your own *mystery*.
Nor should you grieve your language is not fine,
For it is not my best, though it be yours. <note>Vaughan suggests that while the book's language is simple, this was a deliberate choice; he claims to have "voiced" the book in a style that suits the material, even if he is capable of more ornate writing.</note>
I could have voiced you forth in such a dress,
The Spring had been a slut to your expression; <note>In the 17th century, "slut" typically referred to someone untidy or slovenly. Vaughan is saying he could have written so beautifully that the blossoming Spring would look messy and plain by comparison.</note>
Such as might file the rude, unpolished age,
And fix the reader’s soul to every page:
But I have used a coarse, and homely strain,
Because it suits with Truth, which should be plain.
Last, my dear book, if any look on you
As on *Three Suns*, <note>A parhelion or "mock sun." Seeing three suns in the sky was considered a "prodigy"—a supernatural omen or a strange, unsettling phenomenon.</note> or some great prodigy,
And swear to a full point, I do deride
All other sects, to publish my own pride;
Tell such they lie, and since they love not you,
Bid them go learn some *High-shoe* heresy. <note>A "high-shoe" was a sturdy boot worn by peasants. A "high-shoe heresy" refers to the unrefined or vulgar beliefs of the common, uneducated masses.</note>
Nature is not so simple, but she can
Procure a solid reverence from man;
Nor is my pen so lightly plumed that I
Should serve ambition with her majesty.
'Tis Truth makes me come forth, & having writ
This her short *scene*, <note>Vaughan views his writing as a brief performance or "scene" on the stage of natural philosophy.</note> I would not stifle it:
For I have called it *Child*, and I had rather
See it torn by them, than strangled by the Father.

->To God alone be the glory. <note>original: "Soli Deo Gloria."</note><-

->**Amen.**<-

<summary>In this concluding poem, the author defends the unadorned style of his writing as being appropriate for the 'plainness' of Truth, while distancing himself from accusations of pride or ambition. He frames the book as his 'child' which he must release into the world, regardless of how it is received by critics or the unlearned.</summary>
<keywords>Thomas Vaughan, truth, plain style, three suns, high-shoe heresy, Soli Deo Gloria, epilogue, natural philosophy</keywords>

## Taken in a Trap, and tor-
## tur'd to death for
## gnawing the Mar-

### EUGENIUS PHILALETHES <note>A pseudonym for Thomas Vaughan (1621–1666), a Welsh clergyman, philosopher, and alchemist. He used this name to publish his mystical and Hermetic works.</note>

->—*And is Ulysses to be compared with me?*<- <note>original: "Et mecum confertur Vlysses?" This is a quote from Ovid's *Metamorphoses*, spoken by the hero Ajax. Vaughan uses it here to suggest that his opponent—whom he calls the "Man-Mouse"—is unworthy of being compared to him in a contest of wits.</note>

->1 Corinthians 15:32.<-
> *After the manner of men I have fought with Beasts.* <note>Vaughan characterizes his intellectual debate with his critic, Henry More, as a struggle against "beasts" rather than a civilized academic disagreement.</note>

### Printed in LONDON, and Sold
### at the Castle in Cornhill.
### 1650.

<summary>This is the title page of Thomas Vaughan’s biting satirical response to his critic Henry More, framing the academic dispute as a metaphorical trapping and "torture" of a mouse. It establishes the work as a fierce defense of Vaughan's mystical philosophy, Anthroposophia Theomagica.</summary>
